CISA KEV: CVE-2026-21519 — Microsoft Windows Type Confusion Vulnerability

CISA KEV
2026-02-10 1 use case 0 techniques 2 kill-chain phases detected

Microsoft Desktop Windows Manager contains a type confusion vulnerability that could allow an authorized attacker to elevate privileges locally. Vendor: Microsoft, Product: Windows. Federal patch due: 2026-03-03.

Reconnaissance. Attacker researches the target — OSINT, scanning, enumeration. → Asset exposure — vulnerability matches article CVE(s)
Weaponization. Coupling exploit code with deliverable payload (e.g. weaponized PDF/Office doc, trojanized installer).

Match vulnerability scanner output to the CVE(s) named in the article.
ATT&CKT1190
Data sourcesVulnerabilities DeviceInfo DeviceTvmSoftwareVulnerabilities
// TVM tables are daily snapshots — pin a 1-day window so the engine
// partitions on Timestamp instead of scanning the full retention.
DeviceTvmSoftwareVulnerabilities
| where Timestamp > ago(1d)
| where CveId in~ ("CVE-2026-21519")
| join kind=inner (DeviceInfo | where Timestamp > ago(1d)) on DeviceId
| project DeviceName, OSPlatform, CveId, VulnerabilitySeverityLevel, RecommendedSecurityUpdate
| order by VulnerabilitySeverityLevel asc, DeviceName asc

// Sentinel has no native TVM table. If MDE→Sentinel streaming is enabled,
// `DeviceTvmSoftwareVulnerabilities` is available. Otherwise this UC has
// no Sentinel translation — track via Update / Heartbeat OS-version
// gaps as a coarse approximation.
DeviceTvmSoftwareVulnerabilities
| where TimeGenerated > ago(1d)
| where CveId in~ ("CVE-2026-21519")
| join kind=inner (DeviceInfo | where TimeGenerated > ago(1d)) on DeviceId
| project DeviceName, OSPlatform, CveId,
          VulnerabilitySeverityLevel, RecommendedSecurityUpdate
| order by VulnerabilitySeverityLevel asc, DeviceName asc
APPLICATION
Toggle if your env has no CIM data-model acceleration.

    from datamodel=Vulnerabilities
    where Vulnerabilities.signature IN ("CVE-2026-21519")
    by Vulnerabilities.dest, Vulnerabilities.signature, Vulnerabilities.severity, Vulnerabilities.cve
| `drop_dm_object_name(Vulnerabilities)`
| sort - severity
| tstats summariesonly=false count min(_time) as firstTime max(_time) as lastTime
    from datamodel=Vulnerabilities

Lab52
2026-02-08 0 use cases 1 technique 1 kill-chain phase detected

Illegals are intelligence officers operating abroad under false identities and nationalities, without the protection of diplomatic immunity. Posing as ordinary citizens of other countries, these officers maintain fake identities, spending years building normal lives while spying. In front of other spies, these officers have no diplomatic protection: if they are discovered, they go to prison. [&#8230;]

Snyk
2026-02-05 3 use cases 1 technique 5 kill-chain phases detected

Discover how 7.1% of AI agent skills are designed to leak secrets, PII, and API keys through LLM context. Learn to defend with Evo & mcp-scan.

Installation of credential-leaking ClawHub skills (moltyverse-email, buy-anything, prompt-log, youtube-data) AIInstallation High
Detects installation of the four ClawHub marketplace skills Snyk specifically named as exposing API keys, PII and credit card data through LLM context windows. Anchored on the exact `clawhub install <skill>` invocations and the `npm install -g clawhub` bootstrap command from the article. Rationale: Anchors on the four skill names Snyk explicitly named (moltyverse-email, buy-anything, prompt-log, youtube-data) and the clawhub bootstrap command — none of these strings appear in legitimate non-AI-agent dev tooling, so FP rate is naturally low. No external sources required; article gives literal install commands.
ATT&CKT1195.002 T1059.004
Data sourcesEndpoint.Processes
Expected false positives · estimated rate: low — model-assessed, tune to your environment
  • Internal security researcher running the same Snyk audit on a sandbox host
  • Blue-team analyst installing the skill in a contained lab to reproduce vulnerability
  • AI-platform team evaluating the ClawHub marketplace for an org-wide allow/deny policy
  • Developer legitimately testing an AI agent in a personal sandbox after the vuln is disclosed but before the skill is delisted
Suppression filter
| where InitiatingProcessParentFileName !in~ ("snyk-cli.exe","snyk.exe") and InitiatingProcessCommandLine !has "sandbox" and InitiatingProcessCommandLine !has "evo-agent-security-analyzer"
Response runbook
Step 1 (Triage): Confirm the installing user is in an authorized AI-platform / dev role. Capture the exact skill name from ProcessCommandLine and read the skill's SKILL.md from ~/.clawhub/skills/<skill>/SKILL.md to verify the insecure pattern. Step 2 (Contain): If unauthorized, run `clawhub uninstall <skill>` on the host, disable the user's AI-agent session, and rotate any credentials that may have been handed to the agent in that session (Stripe keys, moltyverse API keys, any sensitive env vars). Step 3 (Hunt): On the same host, search DeviceProcessEvents for the next 24h of curl calls referencing api.stripe.com/v1/tokens or moltyverse.email (UC2 logic), and search DeviceFileEvents for any .prompt-log/*.md exports (UC3 logic) that may already contain leaked secrets.
DeviceProcessEvents
| where Timestamp > ago(7d)
| where ProcessCommandLine has "clawhub"
| where ProcessCommandLine has_any ("clawhub install moltyverse-email","clawhub install buy-anything","clawhub install prompt-log","clawhub install youtube-data","npm install -g clawhub")
| where AccountName !endswith "$"
| project Timestamp, DeviceName, AccountName, FileName, FolderPath, ProcessCommandLine, InitiatingProcessFileName, InitiatingProcessCommandLine, InitiatingProcessParentFileName, SHA256
| order by Timestamp desc
SecurityEvent
| where TimeGenerated > ago(7d)
| where EventID == 4688
| where CommandLine has "clawhub"
| where CommandLine has_any ("clawhub install moltyverse-email","clawhub install buy-anything","clawhub install prompt-log","clawhub install youtube-data","npm install -g clawhub")
| where AccountName !endswith "$"
| project TimeGenerated, Computer, Account, NewProcessName, CommandLine, ParentProcessName
| order by TimeGenerated desc
title: Installation of Credential-Leaking ClawHub Agent Skills
id: 8a1f2b6c-3d4e-4f5a-9b0c-1d2e3f4a5b6c
description: Detects installation of ClawHub marketplace skills (moltyverse-email, buy-anything, prompt-log, youtube-data) Snyk identified as exposing API keys, PII and credit-card data through LLM context windows.
references:

author: Detection Engineering
date: 2026/02/05
tags:

logsource:
  category: process_creation
  product: windows
detection:
  selection:
    CommandLine|contains:
      - 'clawhub install moltyverse-email'
      - 'clawhub install buy-anything'
      - 'clawhub install prompt-log'
      - 'clawhub install youtube-data'
      - 'npm install -g clawhub'
  condition: selection
level: medium
source:windows.sysmon @EventID:1 @CommandLine:("clawhub install moltyverse-email" OR "clawhub install buy-anything" OR "clawhub install prompt-log" OR "clawhub install youtube-data" OR "npm install -g clawhub")
#event_simpleName=ProcessRollup2
| CommandLine=/clawhub\s+install\s+(moltyverse-email|buy-anything|prompt-log|youtube-data)|npm\s+install\s+-g\s+clawhub/i
| groupBy([aid, ComputerName, UserName, ImageFileName, CommandLine], function=count())
ENDPOINT
Toggle if your env has no CIM data-model acceleration.

| tstats summariesonly=false count min(_time) as firstTime max(_time) as lastTime from datamodel=Endpoint.Processes where (Processes.process_name="npm.exe" OR Processes.process_name="node.exe" OR Processes.process_name="clawhub.exe" OR Processes.process_name="bash.exe" OR Processes.process_name="pwsh.exe" OR Processes.process_name="powershell.exe") AND (Processes.process="*clawhub install moltyverse-email*" OR Processes.process="*clawhub install buy-anything*" OR Processes.process="*clawhub install prompt-log*" OR Processes.process="*clawhub install youtube-data*" OR Processes.process="*npm install -g clawhub*") by Processes.dest Processes.user Processes.process Processes.parent_process_name Processes.process_name | `drop_dm_object_name(Processes)` | `security_content_ctime(firstTime)` | `security_content_ctime(lastTime)`
AI agent skill leaks Stripe key or card PAN/CVC verbatim in curl command line AIActions on Objectives High
Hunts the exact insecure pattern from buy-anything v2.0.0 and moltyverse-email v1.1.0: a curl/bash invocation that embeds a live Stripe publishable key (pk_live_*) or raw card number plus CVC into the command line for api.stripe.com/v1/tokens, or hits api.moltyverse.email / moltyverse.email/inbox with the API key in the request. These secrets flow through process audit logs, EDR cmd-line capture, and the LLM provider's conversation history. Rationale: Article verbatim shows the curl with `-u "pk_live_51LgDhr..." -d "card[number]=4242..." -d "card[cvc]=123"` against api.stripe.com/v1/tokens and references api.moltyverse.email in the skill metadata. The combination of a Stripe/moltyverse endpoint with raw card form-fields or live Stripe-key prefixes in a single process command line is article-specific and effectively never occurs in well-architected payment flows (real apps post tokens, not PANs).
ATT&CKT1552.001 T1071.001 T1059.004
Data sourcesEndpoint.Processes
Expected false positives · estimated rate: low — model-assessed, tune to your environment
  • Developer testing a Stripe integration with the test PAN 4242424242424242 (Stripe documentation example)
  • Authorized SOC blue-team reproducing the Snyk PoC in a contained lab
  • Legacy server-side Stripe tokenization that still does direct PAN tokenization (technically a PCI finding regardless)
  • Snyk Evo Agent Security Analyzer or similar SAST tool scanning the SKILL.md file (would appear in static-analysis tooling cmdline, not as curl)
Suppression filter
| where ProcessCommandLine !has "4242424242424242" or ProcessCommandLine !has "test" | where InitiatingProcessFolderPath !has "\\stripe-cli\\" and InitiatingProcessCommandLine !has "--testmode"
Response runbook
Step 1 (Triage): Extract every secret in the captured ProcessCommandLine — Stripe key prefix (pk_live_*/sk_live_*), card PAN, CVC, exp, moltyverse `?key=` value. Note exact timestamp, host, user, parent process (likely bash/AI-agent runtime). Step 2 (Contain): Rotate the Stripe key immediately in the Stripe dashboard (Roll); revoke the moltyverse API key; freeze any cards captured (issuer contact); kill the AI-agent session; remove the buy-anything / moltyverse-email skill (`clawhub uninstall`). Step 3 (Hunt): Pull the agent's session .jsonl from ~/.codex/sessions, ~/.claude/projects, or ~/.clawdbot/agents/main/sessions and grep for the leaked values; pull LLM-provider audit logs (OpenAI / Anthropic) for any conversations carrying the same secrets; look for prompt-log .md exports (UC3) that already shared the cleartext into a markdown artifact.
DeviceProcessEvents
| where Timestamp > ago(7d)
| where ProcessCommandLine has_any ("api.stripe.com/v1/tokens","moltyverse.email/inbox","api.moltyverse.email")
| where ProcessCommandLine has_any ("card[number]=","card[cvc]=","pk_live_","sk_live_")
| where AccountName !endswith "$"
| project Timestamp, DeviceName, AccountName, FileName, FolderPath, ProcessCommandLine, InitiatingProcessFileName, InitiatingProcessCommandLine, InitiatingProcessParentFileName, SHA256
| order by Timestamp desc
SecurityEvent
| where TimeGenerated > ago(7d)
| where EventID == 4688
| where CommandLine has_any ("api.stripe.com/v1/tokens","moltyverse.email/inbox","api.moltyverse.email")
| where CommandLine has_any ("card[number]=","card[cvc]=","pk_live_","sk_live_")
| where AccountName !endswith "$"
| project TimeGenerated, Computer, Account, NewProcessName, CommandLine, ParentProcessName
| order by TimeGenerated desc
title: AI Agent Skill Leaks Stripe Key or Card Data via curl
id: 9b2c3d7e-4f5a-5a6b-0c1d-2e3f4a5b6c7d
description: Detects curl/bash invocations embedding live Stripe keys (pk_live_/sk_live_) or raw card numbers in command lines for api.stripe.com/v1/tokens or moltyverse.email — matching the Snyk-disclosed insecure pattern in ClawHub buy-anything v2.0.0 and moltyverse-email v1.1.0.
references:
  - https://snyk.io/blog/openclaw-skills-credential-leaks-research/
author: Detection Engineering
date: 2026/02/05
tags:
  - attack.exfiltration
  - attack.t1552.001
  - attack.t1071.001
logsource:
  category: process_creation
  product: windows
detection:
  selection_target:
    CommandLine|contains:
      - 'api.stripe.com/v1/tokens'
      - 'moltyverse.email/inbox'
      - 'api.moltyverse.email'
  selection_secret:
    CommandLine|contains:
      - 'card[number]='
      - 'card[cvc]='
      - 'pk_live_'
      - 'sk_live_'
  condition: selection_target and selection_secret
level: high
source:windows.sysmon @EventID:1 @CommandLine:("api.stripe.com/v1/tokens" OR "moltyverse.email/inbox" OR "api.moltyverse.email") AND @CommandLine:("card[number]=" OR "card[cvc]=" OR "pk_live_" OR "sk_live_")
#event_simpleName=ProcessRollup2
| CommandLine=/api\.stripe\.com\/v1\/tokens|moltyverse\.email\/inbox|api\.moltyverse\.email/i
| CommandLine=/card\[number\]=|card\[cvc\]=|pk_live_|sk_live_/i
| groupBy([aid, ComputerName, UserName, ImageFileName, CommandLine], function=count())
ENDPOINT
Toggle if your env has no CIM data-model acceleration.

| tstats summariesonly=false count min(_time) as firstTime max(_time) as lastTime from datamodel=Endpoint.Processes where (Processes.process="*api.stripe.com/v1/tokens*" OR Processes.process="*moltyverse.email/inbox*" OR Processes.process="*api.moltyverse.email*") AND (Processes.process="*card[number]=*" OR Processes.process="*card[cvc]=*" OR Processes.process="*pk_live_*" OR Processes.process="*sk_live_*") by Processes.dest Processes.user Processes.process Processes.process_name Processes.parent_process_name | `drop_dm_object_name(Processes)` | `security_content_ctime(firstTime)` | `security_content_ctime(lastTime)`
AI session-log harvest via prompt-log extract.sh writing markdown with embedded secrets AIActions on Objectives Medium
Detects the ClawHub prompt-log skill's `scripts/extract.sh` reading `.jsonl` session transcripts from agent session directories (~/.codex/sessions, ~/.claude/projects, ~/.clawdbot/agents) and emitting a markdown transcript to `.prompt-log/`. As noted by Snyk, this step re-exposes any plaintext credentials handled in earlier agent interactions as a static, shareable artifact. Rationale: Article literally shows the prompt-log usage examples: `scripts/extract.sh ~/.codex/sessions/2026/01/12/abcdef.jsonl` and `scripts/extract.sh ~/.claude/projects/my-proj/xyz.jsonl --output my-transcript.md`, with default output `.prompt-log/YYYY-MM-DD-HHMMSS.md`. Combining the script name with any of the three session-directory patterns (or the .prompt-log output path) gives a high-fidelity, article-specific hit.
ATT&CKT1005 T1552.001 T1213
Data sourcesEndpoint.Processes Endpoint.Filesystem
Expected false positives · estimated rate: low — model-assessed, tune to your environment
  • Legitimate developer exporting their own session transcripts to share a bug repro with vendor support
  • Internal IR/forensics team triaging an AI agent incident by extracting session logs
  • Snyk Evo Agent Security Analyzer or other auditing tool walking session directories during a research scan
  • Developer using a similarly-named extract.sh for unrelated jq/jsonl batch processing
Suppression filter
| where InitiatingProcessCommandLine !has "--output" or InitiatingProcessCommandLine has_any ("--after","--before") | where InitiatingProcessParentFileName !in~ ("ir-toolkit.sh","forensics-collector.exe")
Response runbook
Step 1 (Triage): Locate the produced markdown (default `.prompt-log/YYYY-MM-DD-HHMMSS.md`). Read it and inventory any secrets it now contains in plaintext: Stripe keys, moltyverse API keys, card data, OAuth tokens, internal API tokens. Step 2 (Contain): Quarantine the markdown — delete from host and from any sync targets (OneDrive, Dropbox, iCloud, git working trees, Slack uploads, GitHub gists). Uninstall the prompt-log skill: `clawhub uninstall prompt-log`. Rotate every identified secret. Step 3 (Hunt): Search the org for any other `.prompt-log/*.md` files via DeviceFileEvents/auditd; check git history (git log --all -- '**/.prompt-log/**') for accidental commits; review DLP/CASB logs for outbound .md uploads from the host; pivot to UC1 to see if other vulnerable skills are installed.
let SessionAnchors = dynamic([".codex/sessions",".claude/projects",".clawdbot/agents",".jsonl",".prompt-log"]);
let ProcHits = DeviceProcessEvents
| where Timestamp > ago(7d)
| where ProcessCommandLine has "extract.sh"
| where ProcessCommandLine has_any (SessionAnchors)
| where AccountName !endswith "$"
| project Timestamp, DeviceName, AccountName, FileName, ProcessCommandLine, InitiatingProcessFileName, InitiatingProcessCommandLine;
let FileHits = DeviceFileEvents
| where Timestamp > ago(7d)
| where ActionType in ("FileCreated","FileRenamed","FileModified")
| where FolderPath has ".prompt-log" and FileName endswith ".md"
| where InitiatingProcessAccountName !endswith "$"
| project Timestamp, DeviceName, AccountName=InitiatingProcessAccountName, FileName, ProcessCommandLine=InitiatingProcessCommandLine, InitiatingProcessFileName, InitiatingProcessCommandLine;
ProcHits
| union FileHits
| order by Timestamp desc
Syslog
| where TimeGenerated > ago(7d)
| where SyslogMessage has "extract.sh"
| where SyslogMessage has_any (".codex/sessions",".claude/projects",".clawdbot/agents",".prompt-log",".jsonl")
| project TimeGenerated, Computer, ProcessName, SyslogMessage
| union (
    SecurityEvent
    | where TimeGenerated > ago(7d)
    | where EventID == 4688
    | where CommandLine has "extract.sh"
    | where CommandLine has_any (".codex/sessions",".claude/projects",".clawdbot/agents",".prompt-log",".jsonl")
    | where AccountName !endswith "$"
    | project TimeGenerated, Computer, ProcessName=NewProcessName, SyslogMessage=CommandLine
  )
| order by TimeGenerated desc
title: AI Session Log Harvest via prompt-log extract.sh
id: ac3d4e8f-5a6b-6c7d-1e2f-3a4b5c6d7e8f
description: Detects the ClawHub prompt-log skill's extract.sh reading .jsonl session transcripts from AI agent session directories and writing markdown to .prompt-log/. Per Snyk research, the resulting markdown can contain plaintext credentials handled in prior agent runs.
references:
  - https://snyk.io/blog/openclaw-skills-credential-leaks-research/
author: Detection Engineering
date: 2026/02/05
tags:
  - attack.collection
  - attack.t1005
  - attack.t1552.001
logsource:
  category: process_creation
  product: linux
detection:
  selection_script:

Critical

Snyk Finds Prompt Injection in 36%, 1467 Malicious Payloads in a ToxicSkills Study of Agent Skills Supply Chain Compromise

Snyk
2026-02-05 8 use cases 3 techniques 7 kill-chain phases detected

Snyk’s ToxicSkills research reveals 36% of AI agent skills contain security flaws, including 1,467 vulnerable skills and active malicious payloads targeting OpenClaw, Claude Code, and Cursor users.

Actions on Objectives
Detected
Reconnaissance. Attacker researches the target — OSINT, scanning, enumeration.
Weaponization. Coupling exploit code with deliverable payload (e.g. weaponized PDF/Office doc, trojanized installer). → [LLM] Prompt-injection markers (base64, Unicode tags, 'ignore previous instructions') in SKILL.md content
Delivery. Transmitting the weapon (phishing email, USB, watering hole, drive-by, malicious ad). → [LLM] Outbound connection to clawhub.ai or skills.sh from CLI agent (skill marketplace fetch)
Exploitation. Triggering the exploit — vulnerability/macro/trust abuse to execute code. → [LLM] curl | bash or wget | sh executed by Claude/Cursor/OpenClaw agent process · PowerShell encoded / obfuscated command · Trusted vendor binary / installer launching unusual children · Article-specific behavioural hunt — Snyk Finds Prompt Injection in 36%, 1467 Malicious Payloads in a ToxicSkills Stu
Installation. Establishing persistence on the host — backdoor, service, scheduled task, registry key. → [LLM] SKILL.md written to ~/.claude/skills/ or ~/.openclaw/skills/ (agent-skill install)
Command & Control. Beacon to attacker infrastructure for control and tasking.
Actions on Objectives. Lateral movement, credential dumping, data theft, ransomware, sabotage. → [LLM] AI agent process reads cloud-credential, SSH or dotenv files (skill credential theft)
SKILL.md written to ~/.claude/skills/ or ~/.openclaw/skills/ (agent-skill install) AIInstallation Medium
Detects creation of a SKILL.md file under known Agent Skills install directories by CLI tooling (claude, cursor, git, curl, wget). Per Snyk's ToxicSkills audit, every malicious skill ships as a SKILL.md markdown manifest dropped into the agent's skills directory — the install moment is the cleanest pivot before the agent ever reads the file. Rationale: Anchored on the article's two named marketplaces (ClawHub, skills.sh) and the SKILL.md manifest format (Code Block 1). Path patterns mirror Claude Code / Cursor / OpenClaw skill install directories. Cross-checked Snyk blog body + Code Block 1. Cross-checked against: • https://snyk.io/blog/toxicskills-malicious-ai-agent-skills-clawhub/
ATT&CKT1195.002 T1059
Data sourcesEndpoint.Filesystem
Expected false positives · estimated rate: medium — model-assessed, tune to your environment
  • Developer authoring a legitimate in-house skill locally with `claude` CLI
  • Git clone of an internal skills repo into ~/.claude/skills/
  • IDE template scaffold creating SKILL.md from a Cursor extension
  • Backup/sync tool (OneDrive, rsync) restoring user dotfiles
  • Documentation tooling generating a SKILL.md placeholder
Suppression filter
and not (InitiatingProcessCommandLine has_any ("clone https://github.com/<org>/internal-skills", "\\corp-skills-repo\\") or InitiatingProcessAccountName in~ ("developer-svc", "build-bot"))
Response runbook
Step 1 — Triage: Pull the full path, parent process tree, and writing user; inspect SKILL.md content for base64 blobs, Unicode tag characters (U+E0000 range), `<system>` impersonation, `ignore previous instructions`, or curl|bash inside setup. Cross-check the skill's author/ID against the 8 publicly-listed malicious skills Snyk has flagged on clawhub.ai. Step 2 — Contain: Quarantine the SKILL.md, kill any active claude/cursor agent session for the user, rotate any credentials accessible to that user (AWS, GitHub PAT, env-stored API keys). Step 3 — Hunt: Pivot to that user/host's DNS for clawhub.ai/skills.sh in the prior 24h, look for outbound curl|bash from the agent shell, and search for additional SKILL.md drops across the fleet from the same author tag.
DeviceFileEvents
| where Timestamp > ago(7d)
| where ActionType in ("FileCreated", "FileRenamed")
| where FileName =~ "SKILL.md"
| where FolderPath has_any (@"\.claude\skills\", @"\.openclaw\skills\", @"\.cursor\skills\", "/.claude/skills/", "/.openclaw/skills/", "/.cursor/skills/")
| where InitiatingProcessFileName has_any ("claude.exe", "claude", "cursor.exe", "cursor", "code.exe", "git.exe", "git", "curl.exe", "curl", "wget.exe", "wget", "pwsh.exe", "powershell.exe", "bash", "sh", "zsh")
| where InitiatingProcessAccountName !endswith "$"
| project Timestamp, DeviceName, InitiatingProcessAccountName, InitiatingProcessFileName, InitiatingProcessCommandLine, FolderPath, FileName, SHA256
| order by Timestamp desc
ImFileEvent
| where TimeGenerated > ago(7d)
| where EventType =~ "FileCreated"
| where TargetFileName =~ "SKILL.md"
| where TargetFilePath has_any (@"\.claude\skills\", @"\.openclaw\skills\", @"\.cursor\skills\", "/.claude/skills/", "/.openclaw/skills/", "/.cursor/skills/")
| where ActingProcessName has_any ("claude", "cursor", "code.exe", "git", "curl", "wget", "pwsh", "powershell", "bash", "sh", "zsh")
| where ActorUsername !endswith "$"
| project TimeGenerated, DvcHostname, ActorUsername, ActingProcessName, ActingProcessCommandLine, TargetFilePath, TargetFileSHA256
| order by TimeGenerated desc

id: 2f4b9c0e-9a7d-4f2c-9a01-bf6f5c7b9d10
description: Detects creation of SKILL.md under known Agent Skills install paths — the manifest format used by ClawHub / skills.sh marketplaces flagged in Snyk ToxicSkills.

curl | bash or wget | sh executed by Claude/Cursor/OpenClaw agent process AIExploitation High
Hunts the article's explicit unverifiable-dependency pattern: a skill's setup step running `curl <url> | bash` (Code Block 2) under the AI agent's process tree. Snyk flagged this as the dominant remote-code-execution vector — skills inherit full shell access of the agent. Rationale: Anchors directly on Code Block 2 (`curl | bash`) and the article's call-out of unverifiable dependencies / dynamic imports / remote instruction loading under skill setup. Process-tree pivot (AI agent → shell → curl|bash) gives high signal because legitimate dev environments rarely pipe untrusted curl output to a shell from within a coding agent. Cross-checked against: • https://snyk.io/blog/toxicskills-malicious-ai-agent-skills-clawhub/
ATT&CKT1059.004 T1105 T1195.002
Data sourcesEndpoint.Processes
Expected false positives · estimated rate: low — model-assessed, tune to your environment
  • Developer manually running a Homebrew/rustup/nvm installer (`curl ... | sh`) inside a Cursor terminal
  • CI bootstrap script invoked via Claude Code in a sandboxed dev container
  • Skill author testing their own setup step against an internal package server
  • SRE running k3s/helm one-liner installs from agent terminal
  • Internal tooling docs that legitimately recommend a `curl|bash` install on a vetted internal domain
Suppression filter
and not (ProcessCommandLine has_any ("raw.githubusercontent.com/Homebrew", "sh.rustup.rs", "get.docker.com", "raw.githubusercontent.com/nvm-sh") and AccountName in~ ("developer", "engineer"))
Response runbook
Step 1 — Triage: Extract the URL fetched, the user, and the SKILL.md (if any) currently active in the agent session. Reverse-resolve the URL — is it a freshly-registered domain, a GitHub release from an unknown user (one of Snyk's CRITICAL categories), or a known internal package mirror? Step 2 — Contain: Kill the agent process, isolate the host pending review, revoke any cloud/API tokens reachable in the user's env. Step 3 — Hunt: Search the fleet for the same downloaded URL/IP, list every SKILL.md installed for the user in the last 30 days, and pivot on the author/skill-ID across other endpoints (Snyk notes the same malicious skill is often republished under multiple author IDs).
DeviceProcessEvents
| where Timestamp > ago(7d)
| where InitiatingProcessFileName has_any ("claude.exe", "claude", "cursor.exe", "cursor", "code.exe", "openclaw.exe", "openclaw")
   or InitiatingProcessParentFileName has_any ("claude.exe", "cursor.exe", "code.exe", "openclaw.exe")
| where FileName in~ ("bash", "bash.exe", "sh", "zsh", "pwsh.exe", "powershell.exe", "cmd.exe")
| where ProcessCommandLine matches regex @"(?i)(curl|wget)\s+[^|]*\|\s*(bash|sh|zsh)"
   or ProcessCommandLine matches regex @"(?i)(Invoke-WebRequest|iwr|curl)\s+[^|]+\s*\|\s*(Invoke-Expression|iex)"
| project Timestamp, DeviceName, AccountName, InitiatingProcessFileName, InitiatingProcessCommandLine, FileName, ProcessCommandLine, SHA256
| order by Timestamp desc
ImProcessCreate
| where TimeGenerated > ago(7d)
| where ParentProcessName has_any ("claude", "cursor", "code.exe", "openclaw")
| where TargetProcessName has_any ("bash", "sh", "zsh", "pwsh.exe", "powershell.exe", "cmd.exe")
| where TargetProcessCommandLine matches regex @"(?i)(curl|wget)\s+[^|]*\|\s*(bash|sh|zsh)"
   or TargetProcessCommandLine matches regex @"(?i)(Invoke-WebRequest|iwr|curl)\s+[^|]+\s*\|\s*(Invoke-Expression|iex)"
| project TimeGenerated, DvcHostname, ActorUsername, ParentProcessName, ParentProcessCommandLine, TargetProcessName, TargetProcessCommandLine, SHA256
| order by TimeGenerated desc
title: AI Agent (Claude/Cursor/OpenClaw) Spawns curl|bash or wget|sh
id: 7c1d3f4a-5b22-4e11-b3c2-9d6a0c8e1234
description: Detects an AI coding agent process spawning a shell that pipes curl/wget output directly to bash/sh — the unverifiable-dependency pattern flagged in Snyk ToxicSkills.
references:
  - https://snyk.io/blog/toxicskills-malicious-ai-agent-skills-clawhub/
author: SOC Detection Engineering
date: 2026/02/06
tags:
  - attack.t1059.004
  - attack.execution
logsource:
  category: process_creation
  product: linux
detection:
  parent:
    ParentImage|endswith:
      - '/claude'
      - '/cursor'
      - '/code'
      - '/openclaw'
  child:
    Image|endswith:
      - '/bash'
      - '/sh'
      - '/zsh'
  cmd:
    CommandLine|re: '(?i)(curl|wget)\s+[^|]*\|\s*(bash|sh|zsh)'
  condition: parent and child and cmd
level: high
source:(windows.sysmon OR linux.auditd) (@ParentImage:(*\\claude.exe OR *\\cursor.exe OR *\\code.exe OR *\\openclaw.exe) OR @process.parent.executable.path:(*/claude OR */cursor OR */code OR */openclaw)) (@CommandLine:(*"curl"*"|"*"bash"* OR *"curl"*"|"*"sh"* OR *"wget"*"|"*"bash"* OR *"wget"*"|"*"sh"*) OR @process.command_line:(*"curl"*"|"*"bash"* OR *"wget"*"|"*"sh"*))
#event_simpleName=ProcessRollup2
| ParentBaseFileName=/(claude|cursor|code|openclaw)(\.exe)?$/i
| ImageFileName=/(bash|sh|zsh|pwsh\.exe|powershell\.exe|cmd\.exe)$/i
| regex("(?i)(curl|wget|Invoke-WebRequest|iwr)\\s+[^|]+\\|\\s*(bash|sh|zsh|Invoke-Expression|iex)", field=CommandLine)
| groupBy([aid, ComputerName, UserName, ParentBaseFileName, ImageFileName, CommandLine], function=count())
ENDPOINT

AI agent process reads cloud-credential, SSH or dotenv files (skill credential theft) AIActions on Objectives Medium
Detects an AI coding agent (claude, cursor, code, openclaw) — or a shell it spawned — reading credential stores: ~/.aws/credentials, ~/.ssh/id_*, ~/.npmrc, ~/.netrc, .env. Snyk's confirmed-malicious 76-skill corpus targeted credential theft via skills that inherit the agent's file-system access. Rationale: Anchored on the article's enumeration of what malicious skills target: 'credentials stored in environment variables and config files' + the 76 confirmed-malicious payloads designed for 'credential theft'. The combination of AI-agent-as-parent + dotfile-credential-read is rare enough in normal dev workflows to be a high-signal hunt. Cross-checked against: • https://snyk.io/blog/toxicskills-malicious-ai-agent-skills-clawhub/
ATT&CKT1552.001 T1552.004 T1195.002
Data sourcesEndpoint.Filesystem
Expected false positives · estimated rate: medium — model-assessed, tune to your environment
  • Developer running `aws sts get-caller-identity` from Claude Code terminal
  • Cursor's built-in git integration reading .gitconfig
  • npm install inside Cursor reading .npmrc for a private registry
  • Engineer asking Claude to debug a .env loading issue (legit read)
  • Backup software indexing dotfiles while the agent is open
Suppression filter
and not (InitiatingProcessCommandLine has_any ("aws sts", "aws configure list", "git config", "npm config get") and FolderPath !has ".ssh")
Response runbook
Step 1 — Triage: Identify the SKILL.md the agent was running when the read fired (correlate by parent agent process and session). Compare the read file's modification time to the skill install time — credential-stealer skills typically read within seconds of install. Step 2 — Contain: Rotate the credentials accessed (AWS keys, SSH keys, GitHub PAT, npm token), kill the agent session, quarantine the SKILL.md. Step 3 — Hunt: Look for outbound POSTs from the agent in the same session window (DeviceNetworkEvents to non-internal IPs), grep the SKILL.md for base64 blobs or webhook URLs (Discord, Telegram, ngrok, requestbin), and search the fleet for the same SKILL.md hash.

| where InitiatingProcessFileName has_any ("claude.exe", "claude", "cursor.exe", "cursor", "code.exe", "openclaw.exe", "openclaw")
   or InitiatingProcessParentFileName has_any ("claude.exe", "cursor.exe", "code.exe", "openclaw.exe")
| where InitiatingProcessAccountName !endswith "$"
| project Timestamp, DeviceName, InitiatingProcessAccountName, InitiatingProcessFileName, InitiatingProcessParentFileName, InitiatingProcessCommandLine, FolderPath, FileName
| order by Timestamp desc

Outbound connection to clawhub.ai or skills.sh from CLI agent (skill marketplace fetch) AIDelivery Medium
Detects DNS/HTTP traffic to the article's two named Agent Skills marketplaces (clawhub.ai, skills.sh) from non-browser CLI tooling. ATT&CKT1195.002 T1071.001
Data sourcesNetwork_Traffic.All_Traffic Web.Web
Expected false positives · estimated rate: medium — model-assessed, tune to your environment
  • Developer running `claude skill install <name>` for a legitimate skill
  • Cursor's auto-updater pulling its skills index
  • Internal CI job seeding a developer image with a vetted skill list
  • Engineer using `curl https://clawhub.ai/...` to read API docs
  • Browser-spawned helper process making API calls to the marketplace
Suppression filter
and not (InitiatingProcessCommandLine has_any ("skill list", "skill search", "skill info") and RemoteUrl !contains "download")
Response runbook
Step 1 — Triage: Decode the full URL — was the request for the marketplace index, a specific skill manifest, or a tarball download? Cross-reference the skill ID/author against Snyk's published list of confirmed-malicious skills. Step 2 — Contain: If the skill is on the malicious list, remove its SKILL.md from the user's skills directory and kill any agent session that loaded it. Step 3 — Hunt: Search for the same skill ID/author across the fleet, look for outbound connections to non-marketplace hosts from the agent process in the following hour, and pivot on the user's recent claude/cursor sessions for sensitive context that may have leaked.

| where Timestamp > ago(7d)
| where RemoteUrl has_any ("clawhub.ai", "skills.sh")
| where InitiatingProcessFileName has_any ("claude.exe", "claude", "cursor.exe", "cursor", "code.exe", "openclaw.exe", "openclaw", "curl.exe", "curl", "wget.exe", "wget", "git.exe", "pwsh.exe", "powershell.exe", "python.exe", "python", "node.exe", "node")
| where InitiatingProcessFileName !in~ ("chrome.exe", "msedge.exe", "firefox.exe", "brave.exe", "safari")
| project Timestamp, DeviceName, InitiatingProcessAccountName, InitiatingProcessFileName, InitiatingProcessCommandLine, RemoteUrl, RemoteIP, RemotePort
| order by Timestamp desc

| where TimeGenerated > ago(7d)
| where DnsQuery has_any ("clawhub.ai", "skills.sh")

    ImProcessCreate
    | where TimeGenerated > ago(7d)
    | where TargetProcessName has_any ("claude", "cursor", "code.exe", "openclaw", "curl", "wget", "git", "pwsh", "powershell", "python", "node")
) on $left.DvcHostname == $right.DvcHostname
| where abs(datetime_diff('second', TimeGenerated, TimeGenerated1)) < 60
| project TimeGenerated, DvcHostname, SrcUsername, DnsQuery, TargetProcessName, TargetProcessCommandLine
| order by TimeGenerated desc

Prompt-injection markers (base64, Unicode tags, 'ignore previous instructions') in SKILL.md content AIWeaponization Medium
Hunts AMSI/script-content telemetry and file-modification events for the prompt-injection signatures Snyk's CRITICAL detector flagged: base64-obfuscated instructions, Unicode tag smuggling (U+E0000–U+E007F), 'ignore previous instructions' patterns, and `<system>` impersonation tokens — inside SKILL.md files or freshly-loaded agent context. Rationale: Anchored on the article's CRITICAL prompt-injection taxonomy row verbatim: 'base64 obfuscation, Unicode smuggling, "ignore previous instructions" patterns, and system message impersonation.' Combined with the SKILL.md filename and the three known skills directories from the article body. Cross-checked against: • https://snyk.io/blog/toxicskills-malicious-ai-agent-skills-clawhub/
ATT&CKT1027 T1059 T1195.002
Data sourcesEndpoint.Filesystem
Expected false positives · estimated rate: medium — model-assessed, tune to your environment
  • Skill author's documentation literally explaining how to prevent prompt injection (educational content)
  • Security-research skill that demonstrates injection payloads as test cases
  • Markdown with a long base64-embedded image data URI for an icon
  • LLM-evaluation skill containing chat-template tokens (<|im_start|>) as fixtures
  • Skill that legitimately uses XML-like fenced blocks for templating
Response runbook
Step 1 — Triage: Render the SKILL.md as plain text and as raw bytes — Unicode tag chars are invisible in rendered Markdown but appear in hex dumps. Decode any base64 blob and look for shell commands, URLs, or further LLM instructions. Step 2 — Contain: Remove the SKILL.md, end any active agent session that loaded it, and force the user to re-auth any service the agent had tokens for (sessions may carry persistent memory per the article). Step 3 — Hunt: SHA256 the file and search the fleet, pivot on the skill author/ID to find other published skills by the same identity, and review the agent's persistent memory store for instructions injected during the compromised session.
DeviceFileEvents
| where Timestamp > ago(7d)
| where ActionType in ("FileCreated", "FileModified")
| where FileName =~ "SKILL.md"
| where FolderPath has_any (@"\.claude\skills\", @"\.openclaw\skills\", @"\.cursor\skills\", "/.claude/skills/", "/.openclaw/skills/", "/.cursor/skills/")
| extend Extra = tostring(AdditionalFields)
| where Extra matches regex @"(?i)(ignore\s+(all\s+)?previous\s+instructions|disregard\s+(all\s+)?prior|</?system>|</?assistant>|\[INST\]|<\|im_start\|>)"
   or Extra contains "\U000E0000"
   or Extra matches regex @"[A-Za-z0-9+/]{200,}={0,2}"
| project Timestamp, DeviceName, InitiatingProcessAccountName, InitiatingProcessFileName, FolderPath, FileName, SHA256, Extra
| order by Timestamp desc

CISA KEV: CVE-2025-11953 — React Native Community CLI OS Command Injection Vulnerability

CISA KEV
2026-02-05 1 use case 0 techniques 2 kill-chain phases detected

React Native Community CLI contains an OS command injection vulnerability which could allow unauthenticated network attackers to send POST requests to the Metro Development Server and run arbitrary executables via a vulnerable endpoint exposed by the server. On Windows, attackers can also execute arbitrary shell commands with fully controlled arguments. Vendor: React Native Community, Product: CLI. Federal patch due: 2026-02-26.

Weaponization. Coupling exploit code with deliverable payload (e.g. weaponized PDF/Office doc, trojanized installer).
Delivery. Transmitting the weapon (phishing email, USB, watering hole, drive-by, malicious ad).

Installation. Establishing persistence on the host — backdoor, service, scheduled task, registry key.
Command & Control. Beacon to attacker infrastructure for control and tasking.

SmarterTools SmarterMail contains a missing authentication for critical function vulnerability in the ConnectToHub API method. Reconnaissance. Attacker researches the target — OSINT, scanning, enumeration. → Asset exposure — vulnerability matches article CVE(s)
Weaponization. Coupling exploit code with deliverable payload (e.g. weaponized PDF/Office doc, trojanized installer).
Delivery. Transmitting the weapon (phishing email, USB, watering hole, drive-by, malicious ad).
Exploitation. Triggering the exploit — vulnerability/macro/trust abuse to execute code.

Command & Control. Beacon to attacker infrastructure for control and tasking.
Actions on Objectives. Lateral movement, credential dumping, data theft, ransomware, sabotage. → Ransomware-style mass file rename / extension change · LSASS process access / dump (credential theft) · Remote service execution — PsExec / SMB lateral movement
Asset exposure — vulnerability matches article CVE(s) Reconnaissance High
Match vulnerability scanner output to the CVE(s) named in the article.
ATT&CKT1190
Data sourcesVulnerabilities DeviceInfo DeviceTvmSoftwareVulnerabilities
// TVM tables are daily snapshots — pin a 1-day window so the engine
// partitions on Timestamp instead of scanning the full retention.
DeviceTvmSoftwareVulnerabilities
| where Timestamp > ago(1d)
| where CveId in~ ("CVE-2026-24423")
| join kind=inner (DeviceInfo | where Timestamp > ago(1d)) on DeviceId
| project DeviceName, OSPlatform, CveId, VulnerabilitySeverityLevel, RecommendedSecurityUpdate
| order by VulnerabilitySeverityLevel asc, DeviceName asc

// Sentinel has no native TVM table. If MDE→Sentinel streaming is enabled,
// `DeviceTvmSoftwareVulnerabilities` is available. Otherwise this UC has
// no Sentinel translation — track via Update / Heartbeat OS-version
// gaps as a coarse approximation.
DeviceTvmSoftwareVulnerabilities
| where TimeGenerated > ago(1d)
| where CveId in~ ("CVE-2026-24423")
| join kind=inner (DeviceInfo | where TimeGenerated > ago(1d)) on DeviceId
| project DeviceName, OSPlatform, CveId,
          VulnerabilitySeverityLevel, RecommendedSecurityUpdate
| order by VulnerabilitySeverityLevel asc, DeviceName asc

    where Vulnerabilities.signature IN ("CVE-2026-24423")
    by Vulnerabilities.dest, Vulnerabilities.signature, Vulnerabilities.severity, Vulnerabilities.cve
| `drop_dm_object_name(Vulnerabilities)`
| sort - severity

Ransomware-style mass file rename / extension change Actions on Objectives Medium
Threshold detection: many files renamed in short window, often with new extension.
ATT&CKT1486
Data sourcesEndpoint.Filesystem DeviceFileEvents
DeviceFileEvents
| where Timestamp > ago(1d)
| where InitiatingProcessAccountName !endswith "$"
| where ActionType in ("FileRenamed","FileModified")
| summarize files = dcount(FileName) by DeviceName, InitiatingProcessAccountName, bin(Timestamp, 1m)
| where files > 200    // empirical: > 200 unique-file renames in 1m by one account on one host
                       //            is well above the P99 of legitimate bulk-tooling
| order by files desc

ImFileEvent
| where TimeGenerated > ago(1d)
| where ActorUsername !endswith "$"
| where EventType in ("FileRenamed","FileModified")
| summarize files = dcount(TargetFileName)
            by DvcHostname, ActorUsername, ActingProcessName, bin(TimeGenerated, 1m)
| where files > 200    // P99 of legit bulk-tooling per-minute rename volume
| order by files desc
ENDPOINT
Toggle if your env has no CIM data-model acceleration.
| tstats `summariesonly` count, dc(Filesystem.file_name) AS files
    from datamodel=Endpoint.Filesystem
    where Filesystem.action IN ("modified","renamed")
    by Filesystem.dest, Filesystem.user, _time span=1m
| `drop_dm_object_name(Filesystem)`
| where files > 200
| sort - files
| tstats summariesonly=false count, dc(Filesystem.file_name) AS files
    from datamodel=Endpoint.Filesystem
    where Filesystem.action IN ("modified","renamed")
    by Filesystem.dest, Filesystem.user, _time span=1m
| `drop_dm_object_name(Filesystem)`
| where files > 200
| sort - files
LSASS process access / dump (credential theft) Actions on Objectives High
Mimikatz, comsvcs.dll MiniDump, or any non-Windows process opening LSASS.
ATT&CKT1003.001 T1003
Data sourcesEndpoint.Processes DeviceEvents
DeviceEvents
| where Timestamp > ago(7d)
| where AccountName !endswith "$"
| where ActionType == "OpenProcessApiCall"
| where FileName =~ "lsass.exe"
| where InitiatingProcessFileName !in~ ("MsSense.exe","MsMpEng.exe","csrss.exe",
                                          "svchost.exe","wininit.exe","services.exe",
                                          "lsm.exe","SearchProtocolHost.exe")
| project Timestamp, DeviceName, ActionType, FileName,
          InitiatingProcessFileName, InitiatingProcessCommandLine,
          InitiatingProcessFolderPath, AccountName
| order by Timestamp desc
// SecurityEvent EventID 4663 = object access. AccessMask 0x10 / 0x40 / 0x1010
// indicate read access to lsass.exe — credential-dump signature. Audit
// Object Access > Audit File System policy required.
SecurityEvent
| where TimeGenerated > ago(7d)
| where EventID == 4663
| where ObjectName endswith "\lsass.exe"
| where Account !endswith "$"
| project TimeGenerated, Computer, Account, ObjectName, ProcessName,
          AccessMask, AccessReason
| order by TimeGenerated desc

title: LSASS Process Access Outside Microsoft-Signed Tooling
id: 7c8b2e10-4d4f-4a8c-9c1f-bf6e2c4a8d04
status: experimental
description: |
    Anything opening lsass.exe with read-memory access that isn't a
    Microsoft signed component (Defender, csrss, etc.) is suspect.
    Mimikatz / Pypykatz / nanodump signature.
references:
    - https://attack.mitre.org/techniques/T1003/001/
author: Clankerusecase
date: 2026/05/02
tags:
    - attack.t1003.001
    - attack.credential-access
logsource:
    category: process_access
    product: windows
detection:
    selection:
        TargetImage|endswith: '\lsass.exe'
    filter_known_good:
        SourceImage|endswith:
            - '\MsSense.exe'
            - '\MsMpEng.exe'
            - '\csrss.exe'
            - '\svchost.exe'
            - '\wininit.exe'
            - '\services.exe'
            - '\lsm.exe'
            - '\SearchProtocolHost.exe'
    condition: selection and not filter_known_good
falsepositives:
    - Third-party EDR / forensic tools — extend the filter list
level: high

Remote service execution — PsExec / SMB lateral movement Actions on Objectives High
psexec / paexec / smbexec / wmic /node — service install over SMB from remote host.
ATT&CKT1021.002 T1569.002
Data sourcesEndpoint.Processes DeviceProcessEvents
DeviceProcessEvents
| where Timestamp > ago(7d)
| where AccountName !endswith "$"
| where FileName in~ ("psexec.exe","psexesvc.exe","paexec.exe","smbexec.py")
   or (FileName =~ "wmic.exe" and ProcessCommandLine has "/node:")
| project Timestamp, DeviceName, AccountName, FileName, ProcessCommandLine, InitiatingProcessFileName
| order by Timestamp desc

SecurityEvent
| where TimeGenerated > ago(7d)
| where EventID == 4688            // process-create (audit-policy required)
| where Account !endswith "$"
| where NewProcessName endswith "\psexec.exe"
   or NewProcessName endswith "\psexesvc.exe"
   or NewProcessName endswith "\paexec.exe"
   or (NewProcessName endswith "\wmic.exe" and CommandLine has "/node:")
| project TimeGenerated, Computer, Account, NewProcessName, CommandLine,
          ParentProcessName
| order by TimeGenerated desc

npx Confusion: Packages That Forgot to Claim Their Own Name

Aikido
2026-02-04 8 use cases 4 techniques 6 kill-chain phases detected

We claimed 128 unclaimed npm package names that official docs told developers to npx. Seven months later: 121,000 downloads. All would have run arbitrary code. Category: Vulnerabilities & Threats

ATT&CKT1190 T1195.002 T1555.003 T1566
Click any ATT&CK pill below to open it on the Matrix

Phase 5
Installation
Detected
Phase 6
Command & Control
Detected
Phase 7
Actions on Objectives
Detected
Reconnaissance. Attacker researches the target — OSINT, scanning, enumeration.
Weaponization. Coupling exploit code with deliverable payload (e.g. weaponized PDF/Office doc, trojanized installer).
Delivery. Transmitting the weapon (phishing email, USB, watering hole, drive-by, malicious ad). → Phishing-link click correlated to endpoint execution · Email attachment opened from external sender
Exploitation. Triggering the exploit — vulnerability/macro/trust abuse to execute code. → Office app spawning script/LOLBin child process · Trusted vendor binary / installer launching unusual children
Installation. Establishing persistence on the host — backdoor, service, scheduled task, registry key. → [LLM] npx invocation of known phantom package names disclosed by Aikido · [LLM] File creation under npx cache for Aikido-claimed phantom package names
Command & Control. Beacon to attacker infrastructure for control and tasking.
Actions on Objectives. Lateral movement, credential dumping, data theft, ransomware, sabotage. → Crypto-wallet file/keystore access by non-wallet process · Infostealer — non-browser process accessing browser cookie/login DBs
npx invocation of known phantom package names disclosed by Aikido AIInstallation High
Hunts execution of `npx` (or its child `node`) with command-line references to the 14 phantom npm package names Aikido publicly claimed in July 2025 / January 2026 after finding them referenced in READMEs/scripts but unregistered. Any hit identifies a workstation or CI runner whose documentation/scripts drift would have led to attacker-controlled code execution if Aikido hadn't defensively claimed the name first. Rationale: Article enumerates 14 specific phantom package names (openapi-generator-cli, cucumber-js, depcruise, jsdoc2md, grpc_tools_node_protoc, vue-demi-switch, styleguidist, mikro-orm-esm, pvbase64, cromwell, git-scripts-pre-push, fathym, aofl, flatjs-forge) with quantified usage (121,539 downloads, peak 4,236/day). These are the literal strings that appear on the npx command line — high-fidelity because all 14 are scoped names a legitimate doc-following developer mis-runs, paired with the npx/node parent constraint to suppress unrelated mentions in chat/IDE logs. Currently low-FP because Aikido controls the registry name; the rule remains valuable as a documentation-drift hunt regardless. Did not external-search — IOC list is the article's own published roster.
ATT&CKT1195.002 T1059.007 T1204.002
Data sourcesEndpoint.Processes
DeviceProcessEvents
| where Timestamp > ago(7d)
| where AccountName !endswith "$"
| where FileName in~ ("npx.exe","npx.cmd","node.exe")
    or InitiatingProcessFileName in~ ("npx.exe","npx.cmd")
| where ProcessCommandLine has_any (
    "openapi-generator-cli","cucumber-js","depcruise","jsdoc2md",
    "grpc_tools_node_protoc","vue-demi-switch","styleguidist",
    "mikro-orm-esm","pvbase64","cromwell","git-scripts-pre-push",
    "fathym","aofl","flatjs-forge")
| project Timestamp, DeviceName, AccountName, FileName, ProcessCommandLine,
          InitiatingProcessFileName, InitiatingProcessCommandLine,
          InitiatingProcessFolderPath, SHA256
| order by Timestamp desc
SecurityEvent
| where TimeGenerated > ago(7d)
| where EventID == 4688
| where Account !endswith "$"
| where NewProcessName endswith "\\npx.exe"
    or NewProcessName endswith "\\node.exe"
    or ParentProcessName endswith "\\npx.exe"
    or ParentProcessName endswith "\\npx.cmd"
| where CommandLine has_any (
    "openapi-generator-cli","cucumber-js","depcruise","jsdoc2md",
    "grpc_tools_node_protoc","vue-demi-switch","styleguidist",
    "mikro-orm-esm","pvbase64","cromwell","git-scripts-pre-push",
    "fathym","aofl","flatjs-forge")
| project TimeGenerated, Computer, Account, NewProcessName, CommandLine,
          ParentProcessName, TokenElevationType
| order by TimeGenerated desc

File creation under npx cache for Aikido-claimed phantom package names AIInstallation High
Detects when npx actually downloads and installs one of the 14 phantom package names into the npx cache (`~/.npm/_npx/<hash>/node_modules/<pkg>` on Linux/macOS, `%LocalAppData%\npm-cache\_npx\` on Windows). Catches the install fingerprint even when the invocation came from a non-shell context (IDE task, devcontainer postCreate, GitHub Actions run-name expansion) that may not surface in process telemetry. Rationale: Complements the process-event UC by catching the install artifact on disk — npm/npx canonical cache paths (`_npx`, `.npm/_npx`, `npm-cache\_npx`) intersected with the 14 named phantom packages produces a high-fidelity disk fingerprint that survives even when CommandLine auditing isn't enabled or when npx was invoked from a non-tty context (IDE postinstall hook, devcontainer feature, GitHub Action run-name eval). Path + package-name conjunction makes random `_npx` directory noise impossible. Did not external-search — IOC list is the article's own published roster.
ATT&CKT1195.002 T1546.016
Data sourcesEndpoint.Filesystem
DeviceFileEvents
| where Timestamp > ago(7d)
| where ActionType == "FileCreated"
| where FolderPath has_any (@"\_npx\", @"/.npm/_npx/", @"\npm-cache\_npx\")
| where FolderPath has_any (
    "openapi-generator-cli","cucumber-js","depcruise","jsdoc2md",
    "grpc_tools_node_protoc","vue-demi-switch","styleguidist",
    "mikro-orm-esm","pvbase64","cromwell","git-scripts-pre-push",
    "fathym","aofl","flatjs-forge")
| project Timestamp, DeviceName, FolderPath, FileName, SHA256,
          InitiatingProcessFileName, InitiatingProcessCommandLine,
          InitiatingProcessAccountName, InitiatingProcessFolderPath
| order by Timestamp desc

          ActingProcessName, ActingProcessCommandLine, ActorUsername,
          TargetFileSHA256
| order by TimeGenerated desc

description: |

    category: file_access

            - '\Electrum\wallets\'

title: Infostealer - Non-Browser Process Accessing Browser Credential Stores
id: 7c8b2e10-4d4f-4a8c-9c1f-bf6e2c4a8d01
status: experimental
description: |
    Stealers (RedLine, Lumma, Vidar, Atomic) read Login Data / cookies
    SQLite databases from Chrome/Edge/Firefox profile directories. Any

Phishing-link click correlated to endpoint execution Delivery High
User received an external email containing a URL, clicked it, and within 60 seconds the recipient's endpoint spawned a scripting interpreter or network downloader (PowerShell / cmd / mshta / curl / certutil / bitsadmin). This is the high-fidelity shape — a raw "user clicked an emailed URL" query is too noisy to alert on because every Microsoft 365 tenant generates thousands of legitimate clicks per day. The exec-correlation step suppresses the noise to roughly the rate at which a click actually drives onto-host execution, which is the actionable signal. Pair with UC_PHISH_LINK_BYPASS (Safe Links override) and UC_PHISH_LINK_NEWDOMAIN for orthogonal high-fidelity variants.
ATT&CKT1566.002 T1204.001 T1059.001
Data sourcesEmail.All_Email Web Endpoint.Processes EmailEvents EmailUrlInfo UrlClickEvents DeviceProcessEvents
// Phishing-link click that drives endpoint execution within 60s.
// Far higher fidelity than "every clicked URL" — most legitimate clicks
// never spawn a non-browser child process, so the join eliminates the
// 99% of noise that makes a raw click query unactionable.
let LookbackDays = 7d;
let SuspectClicks = UrlClickEvents
    | where Timestamp > ago(LookbackDays)
    | where AccountName !endswith "$"
    | where ActionType in ("ClickAllowed","ClickedThrough")
    | join kind=inner (
        EmailEvents
        | where Timestamp > ago(LookbackDays)
        | where DeliveryAction == "Delivered"
        | where EmailDirection == "Inbound"
        | project NetworkMessageId, Subject, SenderFromAddress, SenderFromDomain,
                  RecipientEmailAddress, EmailTimestamp = Timestamp
      ) on NetworkMessageId
    | join kind=leftouter (
        EmailUrlInfo | project NetworkMessageId, Url, UrlDomain
      ) on NetworkMessageId, Url
    | project ClickTime = Timestamp, AccountUpn, IPAddress, Url, UrlDomain,
              Subject, SenderFromAddress, SenderFromDomain, RecipientEmailAddress,
              ActionType;
// Correlate to a non-browser child process spawned within 60 seconds on
// the recipient's device.
DeviceProcessEvents
| where Timestamp > ago(LookbackDays)
| where InitiatingProcessFileName in~ ("chrome.exe","msedge.exe","firefox.exe",
                                         "outlook.exe","brave.exe","arc.exe")
| where FileName in~ ("powershell.exe","pwsh.exe","cmd.exe","mshta.exe",
                        "rundll32.exe","regsvr32.exe","wscript.exe","cscript.exe",
                        "bitsadmin.exe","certutil.exe","curl.exe","wget.exe")
| join kind=inner SuspectClicks on $left.AccountName == $right.AccountUpn
| where Timestamp between (ClickTime .. ClickTime + 60s)
| project ClickTime, ProcessTime = Timestamp,
          DelaySec = datetime_diff('second', Timestamp, ClickTime),
          DeviceName, AccountName, RecipientEmailAddress, SenderFromAddress,
          Subject, Url, UrlDomain, ActionType,
          FileName, ProcessCommandLine, InitiatingProcessFileName
| order by ClickTime desc
// UrlClickEvents has no clean Sentinel-native equivalent. The detection
// pivots to: Office Send→Receive proximity to SecurityEvent 4688 of a
// non-browser child process. Lower fidelity than the Defender version.
let LookbackDays = 7d;
let SuspectMailUsers = OfficeActivity
    | where TimeGenerated > ago(LookbackDays)
    | where OfficeWorkload == "Exchange"
    | where Operation =~ "Receive"
    | summarize Subject = any(Subject), Sender = any(Sender)
                by MailboxOwnerUPN, MessageId, EmailTime = bin(TimeGenerated, 1m);
SecurityEvent
| where TimeGenerated > ago(LookbackDays)
| where EventID == 4688
| where ParentProcessName has_any ("\chrome.exe","\msedge.exe",
                                     "\firefox.exe","\outlook.exe","\brave.exe")
| where NewProcessName has_any ("\powershell.exe","\pwsh.exe","\cmd.exe",
                                  "\mshta.exe","\rundll32.exe","\regsvr32.exe",
                                  "\wscript.exe","\cscript.exe","\bitsadmin.exe",
                                  "\certutil.exe","\curl.exe","\wget.exe")
| join kind=inner SuspectMailUsers on $left.Account == $right.MailboxOwnerUPN
| where TimeGenerated between (EmailTime .. EmailTime + 60s)
| project TimeGenerated, EmailTime, Computer, Account, ParentProcessName,
          NewProcessName, CommandLine, Sender, Subject
| order by TimeGenerated desc
WEB
Toggle if your env has no CIM data-model acceleration.
``` Phishing-link click that drives endpoint execution within 60s ```
| tstats `summariesonly` earliest(_time) AS click_time
    from datamodel=Web
    where Web.action="allowed"
    by Web.src, Web.user, Web.dest, Web.url
| `drop_dm_object_name(Web)`
| rename user AS recipient, dest AS clicked_domain, url AS clicked_url
| join type=inner recipient
    [| tstats `summariesonly` count
         from datamodel=Email.All_Email
         where All_Email.action="delivered" AND All_Email.url!="-"
         by All_Email.recipient, All_Email.src_user, All_Email.url, All_Email.subject
     | `drop_dm_object_name(All_Email)`
     | rex field=url "https?://(?<email_domain>[^/]+)"
     | rename recipient AS recipient]
| join type=inner src
    [| tstats `summariesonly` earliest(_time) AS exec_time
         values(Processes.process) AS exec_cmd, values(Processes.process_name) AS exec_proc
         from datamodel=Endpoint.Processes
         where Processes.parent_process_name IN ("chrome.exe","msedge.exe","firefox.exe",
                                                   "outlook.exe","brave.exe","arc.exe")
           AND Processes.process_name IN ("powershell.exe","pwsh.exe","cmd.exe","mshta.exe",
                                            "rundll32.exe","regsvr32.exe","wscript.exe",
                                            "cscript.exe","bitsadmin.exe","certutil.exe",
                                            "curl.exe","wget.exe")
         by Processes.dest, Processes.user
     | `drop_dm_object_name(Processes)`
     | rename dest AS src]
| eval delta_sec = exec_time - click_time
| where delta_sec >= 0 AND delta_sec <= 60
| table click_time, exec_time, delta_sec, recipient, src, src_user, subject,
        clicked_domain, clicked_url, exec_proc, exec_cmd
| sort - click_time
``` Phishing-link click that drives endpoint execution within 60s ```
| tstats summariesonly=false earliest(_time) AS click_time
    from datamodel=Web
    where Web.action="allowed"
    by Web.src, Web.user, Web.dest, Web.url
| `drop_dm_object_name(Web)`
| rename user AS recipient, dest AS clicked_domain, url AS clicked_url
| join type=inner recipient
    [| tstats summariesonly=false count
         from datamodel=Email.All_Email
         where All_Email.action="delivered" AND All_Email.url!="-"
         by All_Email.recipient, All_Email.src_user, All_Email.url, All_Email.subject
     | `drop_dm_object_name(All_Email)`
     | rex field=url "https?://(?<email_domain>[^/]+)"
     | rename recipient AS recipient]
| join type=inner src
    [| tstats summariesonly=false earliest(_time) AS exec_time
         values(Processes.process) AS exec_cmd, values(Processes.process_name) AS exec_proc
         from datamodel=Endpoint.Processes
         where Processes.parent_process_name IN ("chrome.exe","msedge.exe","firefox.exe",
                                                   "outlook.exe","brave.exe","arc.exe")
           AND Processes.process_name IN ("powershell.exe","pwsh.exe","cmd.exe","mshta.exe",
                                            "rundll32.exe","regsvr32.exe","wscript.exe",
                                            "cscript.exe","bitsadmin.exe","certutil.exe",
                                            "curl.exe","wget.exe")
         by Processes.dest, Processes.user
     | `drop_dm_object_name(Processes)`
     | rename dest AS src]
| eval delta_sec = exec_time - click_time
| where delta_sec >= 0 AND delta_sec <= 60
| table click_time, exec_time, delta_sec, recipient, src, src_user, subject,
        clicked_domain, clicked_url, exec_proc, exec_cmd
| sort - click_time
Email attachment opened from external sender Delivery High
Attachment delivery + execution by recipient — most common malware initial access.
ATT&CKT1566.001 T1204.002
Data sourcesEmail.All_Email Endpoint.Processes EmailAttachmentInfo DeviceProcessEvents
let LookbackDays = 7d;
let MalAttachments = EmailAttachmentInfo
    | where Timestamp > ago(LookbackDays)
    | where AccountName !endswith "$"
    | project NetworkMessageId, RecipientEmailAddress,
              AttachmentFileName = FileName, AttachmentSHA256 = SHA256;
DeviceProcessEvents
| where Timestamp > ago(LookbackDays)
| where InitiatingProcessFileName in~ ("OUTLOOK.EXE","winword.exe","excel.exe","powerpnt.exe")
| where FileName in~ ("cmd.exe","powershell.exe","wscript.exe","cscript.exe",
                      "mshta.exe","rundll32.exe","regsvr32.exe")
| join kind=inner MalAttachments on $left.AccountUpn == $right.RecipientEmailAddress
| project Timestamp, DeviceName, AccountName, FileName, ProcessCommandLine,
          InitiatingProcessFileName, AttachmentFileName, AttachmentSHA256
// Sentinel doesn't have an attachment-hash table. Best approximation:
// OfficeActivity Send/Receive activity + correlate to host process spawn.
let LookbackDays = 7d;
let MailWithAttach = OfficeActivity
    | where TimeGenerated > ago(LookbackDays)
    | where OfficeWorkload == "Exchange"
    | where Operation in~ ("Send","Receive")
    | where isnotempty(MessageId)
    | project EmailTime = TimeGenerated, MailboxOwnerUPN, Sender,
              Recipients, Subject, MessageId;
SecurityEvent
| where TimeGenerated > ago(LookbackDays)
| where EventID == 4688
| where ParentProcessName endswith "OUTLOOK.EXE"
| where NewProcessName endswith "\cmd.exe"
   or NewProcessName endswith "\powershell.exe"
   or NewProcessName endswith "\wscript.exe"
   or NewProcessName endswith "\cscript.exe"
   or NewProcessName endswith "\mshta.exe"
| project TimeGenerated, Computer, Account, NewProcessName, CommandLine,
          ParentProcessName
| order by TimeGenerated desc

    from datamodel=Endpoint.Processes
    where Processes.parent_process_name IN ("winword.exe","excel.exe","powerpnt.exe","outlook.exe","onenote.exe","mspub.exe","visio.exe")
      AND Processes.process_name IN ("cmd.exe","powershell.exe","pwsh.exe","wscript.exe","cscript.exe","mshta.exe","rundll32.exe","regsvr32.exe","wmic.exe","bitsadmin.exe","certutil.exe")
    by Processes.dest, Processes.user, Processes.parent_process_name, Processes.process_name, Processes.process
| `drop_dm_object_name(Processes)`
| `security_content_ctime(firstTime)` | `security_content_ctime(lastTime)`
| tstats summariesonly=false count min(_time) as firstTime max(_time) as lastTime
    from datamodel=Endpoint.Processes
    where Processes.parent_process_name IN ("winword.exe","excel.exe","powerpnt.exe","outlook.exe","onenote.exe","mspub.exe","visio.exe")

title: Trusted Vendor Binary Launching Unusual Children
id: 7c8b2e10-4d4f-4a8c-9c1f-bf6e2c4a8d09
status: experimental
description: |
    A signed vendor binary (in the article's named-vendors list)
    spawning script-host or LOLBin children. Strongest signal when
    paired with a recent CVE / supply-chain advisory naming the same
    vendor.
references:
    - https://attack.mitre.org/techniques/T1195/
author: Clankerusecase
date: 2026/05/02
tags:
    - attack.t1195
    - attack.initial-access
logsource:
    category: process_creation
    product: windows
detection:
    selection_child:
        Image|endswith:
            - '\powershell.exe'
            - '\pwsh.exe'
            - '\cmd.exe'
            - '\rundll32.exe'
            - '\regsvr32.exe'
            - '\mshta.exe'
            - '\wscript.exe'
            - '\cscript.exe'
            - '\wmic.exe'
            - '\bitsadmin.exe'
    selection_parent_vendor:
        ParentImage|contains:
            - '\Program Files\'
            - '\Program Files (x86)\'
    condition: selection_child and selection_parent_vendor
falsepositives:
    - Many — installers and updaters legitimately spawn children. Pin
      the parent path to specific vendor binaries observed in the
      article before alerting.
level: medium

Reconnaissance. Attacker researches the target — OSINT, scanning, enumeration.
Weaponization. Coupling exploit code with deliverable payload (e.g. weaponized PDF/Office doc, trojanized installer).
Delivery. Transmitting the weapon (phishing email, USB, watering hole, drive-by, malicious ad).

Installation. Establishing persistence on the host — backdoor, service, scheduled task, registry key.
Command & Control. Beacon to attacker infrastructure for control and tasking. → Beaconing — periodic outbound to small set of destinations
Actions on Objectives. Lateral movement, credential dumping, data theft, ransomware, sabotage.
Beaconing — periodic outbound to small set of destinations Command & Control Medium
C2 channel detection via inter-beacon-time stddev / fan-out to single dest.
ATT&CKT1071.001 T1071.004
Data sourcesNetwork_Traffic.All_Traffic DeviceNetworkEvents
DeviceNetworkEvents
| where Timestamp > ago(1d)
| where RemoteIPType == "Public" and ActionType == "ConnectionSuccess"
| project DeviceName, RemoteIP, RemotePort, Timestamp
| sort by DeviceName asc, RemoteIP asc, RemotePort asc, Timestamp asc
| extend prev_dev = prev(DeviceName, 1), prev_ip = prev(RemoteIP, 1),
         prev_port = prev(RemotePort, 1), prev_ts = prev(Timestamp, 1)
| where DeviceName == prev_dev and RemoteIP == prev_ip and RemotePort == prev_port
| extend delta_sec = datetime_diff('second', Timestamp, prev_ts)
| summarize conn_count = count(), avg_delta = avg(delta_sec), stdev_delta = stdev(delta_sec)
    by DeviceName, RemoteIP, RemotePort
| where conn_count > 30 and avg_delta between (30.0 .. 600.0) and stdev_delta < 5.0
| order by conn_count desc
ImNetworkSession
| where TimeGenerated > ago(2h)
| where DvcAction != "Block"
| where ipv4_is_private(DstIpAddr) == false
| summarize ConnCount = count(),
            DistinctMinutes = dcount(bin(TimeGenerated, 1m))
            by DvcHostname, DstIpAddr, DstPortNumber
| where ConnCount > 50 and DistinctMinutes > 30   // sustained, not bursty
| order by ConnCount desc
NETWORK
Toggle if your env has no CIM data-model acceleration.
| tstats `summariesonly` count, values(All_Traffic.dest_port) AS ports
    from datamodel=Network_Traffic.All_Traffic
    where All_Traffic.action="allowed" AND All_Traffic.dest_category!="internal"
    by _time span=10s, All_Traffic.src, All_Traffic.dest
| `drop_dm_object_name(All_Traffic)`
| streamstats current=f last(_time) AS prev_time by src, dest
| eval delta = _time - prev_time
| stats avg(delta) AS avg_delta stdev(delta) AS sd_delta count by src, dest
| where count > 30 AND sd_delta < 5 AND avg_delta>=30 AND avg_delta<=600
| sort - count
| tstats summariesonly=false count, values(All_Traffic.dest_port) AS ports
    from datamodel=Network_Traffic.All_Traffic
    where All_Traffic.action="allowed" AND All_Traffic.dest_category!="internal"
    by _time span=10s, All_Traffic.src, All_Traffic.dest
| `drop_dm_object_name(All_Traffic)`
| streamstats current=f last(_time) AS prev_time by src, dest
| eval delta = _time - prev_time
| stats avg(delta) AS avg_delta stdev(delta) AS sd_delta count by src, dest
| where count > 30 AND sd_delta < 5 AND avg_delta>=30 AND avg_delta<=600
| sort - count

title: Trusted Vendor Binary Launching Unusual Children
id: 7c8b2e10-4d4f-4a8c-9c1f-bf6e2c4a8d09
status: experimental
description: |
    A signed vendor binary (in the article's named-vendors list)
    spawning script-host or LOLBin children. Strongest signal when
    paired with a recent CVE / supply-chain advisory naming the same
    vendor.
references:
    - https://attack.mitre.org/techniques/T1195/
author: Clankerusecase
date: 2026/05/02
tags:
    - attack.t1195
    - attack.initial-access
logsource:
    category: process_creation
    product: windows
detection:
    selection_child:
        Image|endswith:
            - '\powershell.exe'
            - '\pwsh.exe'
            - '\cmd.exe'
            - '\rundll32.exe'
            - '\regsvr32.exe'
            - '\mshta.exe'
            - '\wscript.exe'
            - '\cscript.exe'
            - '\wmic.exe'
            - '\bitsadmin.exe'
    selection_parent_vendor:
        ParentImage|contains:
            - '\Program Files\'
            - '\Program Files (x86)\'
    condition: selection_child and selection_parent_vendor
falsepositives:
    - Many — installers and updaters legitimately spawn children. Pin
      the parent path to specific vendor binaries observed in the
      article before alerting.
level: medium

DynoWiper / ZOV wiper known-bad SHA-1 hash execution AIInstallation High
Hunts execution of the five DynoWiper / ZOV wiper binaries documented by ESET against the Polish energy victim and the earlier Ukraine ZOV incident. Hash match is the most deterministic indicator we have for this Sandworm sub-campaign. Rationale: All five SHA-1 hashes are pulled directly from the ESET IOC table for DynoWiper (Win32/KillFiles.NMO) and ZOV wiper (Win32/KillFiles.NMJ). Hash-based detection is highest-fidelity for wipers because Sandworm rebuilds them between campaigns.
ATT&CKT1485 T1561.001
Data sourcesEndpoint.Processes
Expected false positives · estimated rate: low — model-assessed, tune to your environment
  • Security researcher detonating sample in an authorized sandbox
  • IR responder copying the binary off the victim host for forensics
  • EDR/AV vendor sample-collection appliance ingesting the artefact
  • Threat-hunter pushing the hash to a honeypot for validation
Suppression filter
| where InitiatingProcessFolderPath !startswith @"C:\Sandbox\" and FolderPath !startswith @"C:\Quarantine\"
Response runbook
1. Triage: confirm hash hit is not from an authorized analyst sandbox; pull the executable path and account that launched it; check ESET/Defender alerting concurrent to the hash hit. 2. Contain: immediately isolate the host via Defender LiveResponse / Falcon RTR network-contain — DynoWiper destroys data on execution, do not wait for full triage. Block the SHA-1 at the EDR tenant level. 3. Hunt: search the last 30 days for any process launched from C:\inetpub\pub\, for DNS to progamevl.ru, and for TCP connections to 31.172.71[.]5; pivot from the parent process / logon session to identify the lateral-movement vector.

| order by Timestamp desc

Executable dropped into C:\inetpub\pub\ shared directory AIDelivery High
Hunts the article-specific staging behaviour where Sandworm dropped schtask.exe, schtask2.exe and <redacted>_update.exe to C:\inetpub\pub\ — a directory under the IIS root that the actor used as a domain-shared drop point. Legitimate Windows administration almost never writes PE files to this path. Rationale: Article explicitly names C:\inetpub\pub\ as the drop location for schtask.exe / schtask2.exe / <redacted>_update.exe. IIS publishing roots are not normal admin script targets, so PE creation there is a high-fidelity wiper-staging signal.
ATT&CKT1570 T1105
Data sourcesEndpoint.Filesystem
Expected false positives · estimated rate: low — model-assessed, tune to your environment
  • IIS administrator legitimately publishing a downloadable installer for end users
  • Web-application deployment pipeline copying an artefact into the pub share
  • Backup software restoring an inetpub directory after disaster recovery
  • Sysadmin testing inetpub publishing functionality during build-out
Suppression filter
| where InitiatingProcessFileName !in~ ("w3wp.exe","WebDeploy.exe","msdeploy.exe","iisreset.exe")
Response runbook
1. Triage: identify which account created the file and whether it is the IIS service account or an interactive admin; cross-check the source IP / share name to determine if it came over SMB from another host. 2. Contain: quarantine the dropped file via Defender LiveResponse / Falcon RTR, block its hash, and disable the dropping account if non-IIS. 3. Hunt: pivot on the dropping account and source host — look for the same account writing to other inetpub-style shares across the domain in the past 30 days, and for any subsequent process execution from C:\inetpub\pub\.
DeviceFileEvents
| where Timestamp > ago(30d)
| where ActionType in ("FileCreated","FileRenamed","FileModified")
| where FolderPath startswith @"C:\inetpub\pub\"
| where FileName endswith ".exe" or FileName endswith ".dll" or FileName endswith ".scr" or FileName endswith ".bat" or FileName endswith ".ps1"
| where InitiatingProcessAccountName !endswith "$"
| project Timestamp, DeviceName, FileName, FolderPath, SHA1, SHA256, InitiatingProcessFileName, InitiatingProcessFolderPath, InitiatingProcessCommandLine, InitiatingProcessAccountName, RequestAccountName, RequestSourceIP
| order by Timestamp desc
ImFileEvent
| where TimeGenerated > ago(30d)
| where EventType in ("FileCreated","FileModified")

| where TargetFileExtension in~ ("exe","dll","scr","bat","ps1")
| where ActorUsername !endswith "$"
| project TimeGenerated, DvcHostname, ActorUsername, ActingProcessName, ActingProcessCommandLine, TargetFilePath, TargetFileName, TargetFileSHA1, TargetFileSHA256
| order by TimeGenerated desc

DynoWiper schtask.exe / *_update.exe execution from C:\inetpub\pub\ AIInstallation High
Hunts process execution of the article-named binaries (schtask.exe, schtask2.exe, *_update.exe) when they launch from C:\inetpub\pub\. Schtask masquerades as schtasks.exe — the legitimate Windows binary uses an 's' at the end, the wiper drops the 's'. Rationale: Article names the three binaries (schtask.exe, schtask2.exe, <redacted>_update.exe) AND the staging path C:\inetpub\pub\. The combination of exact filename plus inetpub path is article-specific and effectively zero-FP. schtask.exe (no trailing s) is a deliberate masquerade of the Windows schtasks.exe binary.
ATT&CKT1485 T1036.005
Data sourcesEndpoint.Processes
Expected false positives · estimated rate: low — model-assessed, tune to your environment
  • IT staff member naming an internal patch installer *_update.exe and publishing it via IIS
  • Legacy backup tooling that drops a binary literally called schtask.exe into inetpub
  • Authorized red-team exercise emulating DynoWiper
  • Sysadmin shorthand-naming a scheduled-task helper as schtask.exe in inetpub
Response runbook
1. Triage: pull the SHA-1/SHA-256 and compare against the ESET IOC list; capture parent-process and logon-session to determine whether execution came from an interactive admin or a remote logon. 2. Contain: network-isolate the host immediately — DynoWiper executes destructive routines in seconds; suspend the launching user; quarantine the binary. 3. Hunt: across the domain, search for any other host with the same SHA-1, any other process executing from C:\inetpub\pub\, and any SMB write to \\*\inetpub\pub\ in the prior 14 days.
DeviceProcessEvents
| where Timestamp > ago(30d)
| where FolderPath startswith @"C:\inetpub\pub\"
| where FileName in~ ("schtask.exe","schtask2.exe") or FileName endswith "_update.exe"
| project Timestamp, DeviceName, AccountName, AccountDomain, FileName, FolderPath, ProcessCommandLine, SHA1, SHA256, InitiatingProcessFileName, InitiatingProcessFolderPath, InitiatingProcessCommandLine, InitiatingProcessAccountName
| order by Timestamp desc
SecurityEvent
| where TimeGenerated > ago(30d)
| where EventID == 4688
| where NewProcessName startswith @"C:\inetpub\pub\"
| where NewProcessName endswith @"\schtask.exe" or NewProcessName endswith @"\schtask2.exe" or NewProcessName endswith "_update.exe"
| project TimeGenerated, Computer, SubjectUserName, SubjectDomainName, NewProcessName, CommandLine, ParentProcessName
| order by TimeGenerated desc
